<sup>24</sup> And know whether the way of wickedness be in me, And lead me in the way of this life.

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 140</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*To the Chief Musician. A Psalm of David*</span></p>

<sup>1</sup> Deliver me, O Jehovah! from the evil man: From the man of injuries do thou preserve me.

<sup>2</sup> Who devise mischiefs in *their* heart; Daily do they congregate for wars.

<sup>3</sup> Sharpened have they their tongue like a serpent: The poison of an asp is under their lips. Selah.

<sup>4</sup> Keep me, O Jehovah! from the hands of the ungodly: From the man of injuries preserve me; Who plot to overthrow my goings.

<sup>5</sup> Laid have the proud a snare for me: And with cords they have spread a net: By the wayside traps have they set for me. Selah.

<sup>6</sup> I said, O Jehovah! my God are thou: Listen, O Jehovah! to the voice of my supplication.

<sup>7</sup> O Jehovah, my Lord! the strength of my salvation, Thou hast put a covering on my head in the day of arms.

<sup>8</sup> Grant not, O Jehovah! the desires of the ungodly man: They have devised, consummate thou not, they shall be exalted. Selah.

<sup>9</sup> [*As for*] the head of those who encompass me, Let the mischief of his lips cover him.

<sup>10</sup> Fall upon them let coals with fire: He shall cast them into deep pits, They shall not rise again.

<sup>11</sup> The man of tongue shall not be established in the earth: The man of violence, evil shall hunt him to banishments.

<sup>12</sup> I have known that accomplish will Jehovah the judgment of the poor one, The judgment of the afflicted one.

<!-- PAGE 1032 -->

<sup>13</sup> Surely the righteous ones will celebrate thy name, Dwell shall the upright ones before thy face.

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 141</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*A Psalm of David*</span></p>

<sup>1</sup> O Jehovah! I have cried to thee: Haste thou to me: Listen to my voice when I cry to thee.

<sup>2</sup> Directed let my prayer be as incense before thy face: [*Let*] the lifting up of my hands [*be*] as the sacrifice of the evening.

<sup>3</sup> Set, O Jehovah! a watch to my mouth; Keep a guard upon the door of my lips.

<sup>4</sup> Incline not my heart to an evil thing, To commit works of ungodliness with the men who work iniquity; And let me not eat of their dainties.

<sup>5</sup> Smite me let the righteous man, [*it shall be*] a kindness; And let him chastise me, [*it shall be*] an oil that is precious, It shall not break my head: For yet also my prayer *shall be* in their calamities.

<sup>6</sup> Cast down on stony places have been their judges: And they shall hear my words, for they are sweet.

<sup>7</sup> As he who breaketh and cutteth on the earth, Scattered have been our bones at the mouth of the grave.

<sup>8</sup> Because to thee, O Jehovah! my Lord! *are* my eyes, In thee I have hoped: do not leave destitute my soul.

<sup>9</sup> Keep me from the hands of the snare which they have spread for me. From the nets of those who work iniquity.

<sup>10</sup> Fall into his nets let the ungodly ones together, Until I escape.

<!-- PAGE 1033 -->

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 142</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*Maschil of David, when he was in the cave. A Prayer*</span></p>

<sup>1</sup> With my voice to, Jehovah I cried; With my voice to. Jehovah I made supplication.

<sup>2</sup> I poured out before his face my meditation; My affliction before his face I declare.

<sup>3</sup> When perplexed within me was my spirit, And thou knewest my path: In the way wherein I walked, they laid a snare for me.

<sup>4</sup> On looking to the right hand, and beholding, None there was who would know me, Perish did refuge from me, [*There was*] no man seeking after my soul.

<sup>5</sup> I cried to thee, O Jehovah! I said, Thou *art* my hope, My portion in the land of the living.

<sup>6</sup> Attend to my cry, For under affliction I labor exceedingly: Deliver me from my persecutors, For they are too strong for me.

<sup>7</sup> Rescue from prison my soul, That I may celebrate thy name: Crown me shall the righteous ones, For thou shalt recompense me.

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 143</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*A Psalm of David*</span></p>

<sup>1</sup> O Jehovah! hear my prayer; Give ear to my supplication; In thy truth answer me, in thy righteousness.

<sup>2</sup> And enter not into judgment with thy servant, For there shall not be justified in thy sight any living man.

<sup>3</sup> For persecuted hath the enemy my soul; He hath prostrated on the earth my life; He hath set me in dark *places*, as the dead of an age.

<!-- PAGE 1034 -->

<sup>4</sup> And perplexed within me is my spirit Within me astonished is my heart.

<sup>5</sup> I remembered the days of old, I meditated on all thy doings; Upon the work of thy hands I meditated.

<sup>6</sup> I spread out my hearts to thee; My soul, as the earth without water, *is* to thee. Selah.

<sup>7</sup> Hasten, answer me, O Jehovah! Fail doth my spirit: Hide not thy face from me, Because I shall be like to those who descend into the pit.

<sup>8</sup> Cause me to hear in the morning thy mercy, For in thee have I hoped: Show me the way in which I may walk, For to thee have I lifted up my soul.

<sup>9</sup> Deliver me from my enemies, O Jehovah! With thee have I hidden [*myself*.] <sup>10</sup> Teach me to do thy will, For thou *art* my God: Let thy good Spirit lead me into the right land.

<sup>11</sup> Because of thy name, O Jehovah I thou wilt quicken me: In thy righteousness bring out of trouble my soul.

<sup>12</sup> And in thy mercy thou wilt scatter my enemies, And wilt destroy all those who afflict my soul; Because I *am* thy servant.

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 144</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*Of David*</span></p>

<sup>1</sup> Blessed *be* Jehovah, my strength, Who teacheth my hands for the fight, My fingers for the battle.

<sup>2</sup> My goodness, and my fortress, My citadel, my deliverer for me, My shield, and in him I have hoped, Who subdueth my people under me.

<!-- PAGE 1035 -->

<sup>3</sup> O Jehovah! what is man that thou acknowledgest him? The son of man that thou thinkest of him?

<sup>4</sup> Man to vanity is like, His days [*are*] as a shadow that is passing.

<sup>5</sup> O Jehovah! bow thy heavens and descend: Touch the mountains and they shall smoke.

<sup>6</sup> Thunder forth thunderings and scatter them; Shoot out thy arrows, and destroy them.

<sup>7</sup> Send thy hand from on high; Rescue me and deliver me from waters great, From the hand of the sons of the alien.

<sup>8</sup> For their mouth hath spoken falsehood, And their right hand is a right hand of deceit.

<sup>9</sup> O God! a song that is new I will sing to thee: Upon the nablum, upon the psaltery I will sing psalms to thee <sup>10</sup> Who givest salvation to kings, Who redeemest David his servant from the hurtful sword.

<sup>11</sup> Redeem me, and rescue me, From the hand of the sons of the alien, Whose mouth hath spoken falsehood, And their right hand *is* a right hand of deceit.

<sup>12</sup> Because our sons *are* as plants Which have grown up in their youth; Our daughters as corners polished After the similitude of a palace.

<sup>13</sup> Our recesses full, Supplying all kinds of good things. Our sheep bringing forth thousands, [*Yea*] ten thousands in our streets.

<sup>14</sup> Our oxen to the burden accustomed; No breach, nor going out, Nor cry in our streets.

<sup>15</sup> Happy the people to whom it is so, To whom Jehovah is their God.

<!-- PAGE 1036 -->

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 145</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*Praise of David*</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><sup>1</sup> I will exalt thee, my God, *my* King! And will bless thy name for ever and ever.</p>

<sup>2</sup> Every day will I bless thee, And praise thy name for ever and ever.

<sup>3</sup> Great *is* Jehovah, and to be praised greatly, And his greatness is incomprehensible.

<sup>4</sup> Generation to generation shall praise thy works, And thy power they shall declare.

<sup>5</sup> Upon the majesty of the glory of thy excellence, And upon the words of thy wonders, I will meditate.

<sup>6</sup> And the might of thy terrible *works* shall they recount; And thy greatness I will declare.

<sup>7</sup> The memory of the greatness of thy goodness they shall speak forth; And of thy righteousness they shall sing.

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><sup>8</sup> Gracious and compassionate *is* Jehovah, Slow to anger and great in clemency.</p>

<sup>9</sup> Good *is* Jehovah to all, And his compassions *are* over all his works.

<sup>10</sup> Celebrate thee, O Jehovah! shall all thy works, And thy meek ones shall bless thee.

<sup>11</sup> Of the glory of thy kingdom they shall tell, And of thy power they shall speak:

<sup>12</sup> To make known to the sons of men his mighty acts, And the glory of the beauty of his kingdom.

<sup>13</sup> Thy kingdom *is* a kingdom of all ages, And thy dominion is throughout generations of generations.

<!-- PAGE 1037 -->

<sup>14</sup> Uphold doth Jehovah all the falling, He raiseth up all who are bowed down.

<sup>15</sup> The eyes of all in thee do hope, And thou givest them their food in its season.

<sup>16</sup> Thou openest thy hand And satisfiest every living *creature* by thy good pleasure.

<sup>17</sup> Righteous *is* Jehovah in all his ways, And merciful in all his works.

<sup>18</sup> Near *is* Jehovah to all who call upon him, To all who call upon him in truth.

<sup>19</sup> The desire of those who fear him he will perform, And their cry he will hear, told he will save then,.

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><sup>20</sup> Keep doth Jehovah all who love him, And all the wicked he will destroy.</p>

<sup>21</sup> The praise of Jehovah speak shall my mouth: And bless shall all flesh his holy name for ever and ever.

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 146</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*Hallelujah*</span></p>

<sup>1</sup> Praise, O my soul! Jehovah.

<sup>2</sup> I will praise Jehovah in my life; I will sing psalms to my God, while I have being.

<sup>3</sup> Confide not in princes, *Nor* in the son of man, in whom there is not help.

<sup>4</sup> Go forth shall his breath, He shall return to his earth, In that day perish shall his thoughts.

<sup>5</sup> Blessed *is* he who hath for his help the God of Jacob; Whose hope is in Jehovah, his God:

<!-- PAGE 1038 -->

<sup>6</sup> Who made heaven and earth, The sea, and all that is in them: Who keepeth truth for ever:

<sup>7</sup> Who doeth judgment for those who are unrighteously oppressed: Who giveth bread to the hungry: Jehovah looseth the prisoners;

<sup>8</sup> Jehovah enlighteneth the blind; Jehovah raiseth up those who are bowed down; Jehovah loveth the righteous;

<sup>9</sup> Jehovah keepeth the strangers; The orphan and the widow he succoureth; And the way of the ungodly he will destroy.

<sup>10</sup> Reign shall Jehovah for ever: Thy God, O Zion, *is* from generation to generation. Hallelujah.

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 147</span></p>

<sup>1</sup> Praise ye God; For it is a good *thing* to sing psalms to our God, For *it is* pleasant, comely *is* praise.

<sup>2</sup> Build up Jerusalem doth Jehovah, The dispersed of Israel he will gather.

<sup>3</sup> He healeth the contrite in heart; He bindeth their sorrows. <span style="color:#800000">ff156</span> <sup>4</sup> He counteth the multitude of the stars: To each of them names doth he give.

<sup>5</sup> Great is our Lord, and abundant in power, His understanding is infinite.

<sup>6</sup> Raise up the afflicted ones doth Jehovah, He casteth down the ungodly ones to the ground.

<sup>7</sup> Sing to Jehovah in praise, Sing psalms to our God upon the harp.

<!-- PAGE 1039 -->
<sup>8</sup> Who covereth the heavens with clouds, Who prepareth for the earth rain, Who maketh to spring up grass upon the mountains, <sup>9</sup> Who giveth to the cattle their food, To the young ones  <span style="color:#800000">ff157</span> of the ravens which cry to him.

<sup>10</sup> Nor in the strength of the horse will he take pleasure, Nor in the legs of man will he delight.

<sup>11</sup> Delight doth Jehovah in those who fear him, [*In those*] who hope in his mercy.

<sup>12</sup> Celebrate, O Jerusalem! Jehovah; Praise thy God, O Zion!

<sup>13</sup> For he strengtheneth the bars of thy gates; He blesseth thy children in the midst of thee.

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><sup>14</sup> Who maketh in thy borders peace; With the fatness of wheat he satisfieth thee.</p>

<sup>15</sup> When he sendeth forth his word upon the earth, Very swiftly run doth his word.

<sup>16</sup> Who giveth snow *white* as wool; The hoarfrost as ashes he scattereth.

<sup>17</sup> He casteth forth his ice as morsels: Before his cold who shall stand?

<sup>18</sup> He shall send his word: red shall melt them: Blow shall his breath, *and* flow shall the waters.

<sup>19</sup> He announceth his words to Jacob, his statutes and his judgments to Israel.

<sup>20</sup> He hath not done so to every nation, And his judgments he hath not made known to them. Hallelujah.

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 148</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*Hallelujah*</span></p>

<sup>1</sup> Praise ye Jehovah from the heavens; Praise him in the heights.

<sup>2</sup> Praise him, all ye his angels! Praise him, all ye his armies!

<!-- PAGE 1040 -->

<sup>3</sup> Praise him, ye Sun and Moon! Praise him, all ye shining stars!

<sup>4</sup> Praise him, ye heavens of heavens! And ye waters above the heavens!

<sup>5</sup> Let them praise the name of Jehovah; For he commanded and they were created:

<sup>6</sup> And he established them for ever: For ever he hath set for them a decree, and it shall not pass beyond.

<sup>7</sup> Praise Jehovah, ye *creatures* from the earth! Dragons, and all deeps!

<sup>8</sup> Fire and hail, snow and ice! Wind of storm, executing his word!

<sup>9</sup> Mountains, and all hills! The tree bearing fruit, and all cedars!

<sup>10</sup> Wild beasts, and all cattle! Reptile, and winged birds!

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><sup>11</sup> Kings of the earth, and all peoples! Princes, and all judges of the earth!</p>

<sup>12</sup> Young men, and also maidens! Old men with children!

<sup>13</sup> Let them praise the name of Jehovah; For high is his name alone; His praise is above the earth and heavens.

<sup>14</sup> And he hath exalted the horn of his people: Praise *is* to all his merciful ones, To the children of Israel, A people which is near to him. Hallelujah.

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 149</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*Hallelujah*</span></p>

<sup>1</sup> Sing ye to Jehovah a song that is new: His praise *is* in the assembly of the merciful ones.

<sup>2</sup> Rejoice let Israel in his Maker; Let the children of Zion exult in their king:

<!-- PAGE 1041 -->

<sup>3</sup> Let them praise his name on the pipe: On the timbrel and the harp let them sing psalms to him.

<sup>4</sup> For taken pleasure hath Jehovah in his people: He will glorify the poor ones unto salvation.

<sup>5</sup> Exult let the merciful ones in glory; Let them shout with joy on their couches.

<sup>6</sup> The exaltations of God are in their mouth, And a sword of two edges in their hand;

<sup>7</sup> To execute vengeance on the heathen, Chastisements on the nations:

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><sup>8</sup> To bind their kings with chains, And their nobles with fetters of iron:</p>

<sup>9</sup> To execute upon them the judgment written. This honor is to all his merciful ones. Hallelujah.

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 150</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*Hallelujah*</span></p>

<sup>1</sup> Praise God in his sanctuary; Praise him in the firmament of his power.

<sup>2</sup> Praise him in his might; Praise him for the multitude of his greatness:

<sup>3</sup> Praise him with sound of trumpet; Praise him with nablum and harp.

<sup>4</sup> Praise him with timbrel and pipe; Praise him with strings and organ.

<sup>5</sup> Praise him with cymbals of sound; Praise him with cymbals of shouting.

<sup>6</sup> Let every thing that breatheth praise God. Hallelujah.

<p class="title-block-h1" style="text-align:center; font-size:22px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#0000d4">TABLE</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">OF THE PARTICULAR SUBJECTS OF EACH PSALM ACCORDING TO CALVIN’S INTERPRETATION.</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">Psalms sung at the Passover, <sup>111</sup>-<sup>118</sup></p>

The Believer’s Scruples, arising from the Prosperity of the Wicked, removed by Revealed Religion and the considerations of their Latter End, <sup>73</sup>. — sung at the Feast of Trumpets, <sup>81</sup>

Meditations of the Believer, in which he encourages himself and others to hope in God, and fortifies his mind against the Assaults of temptation, <sup>62</sup>. — sung at the Feast of Tabernacles, <sup>65</sup> <sup>67</sup>

The high value which the Believer sets on God’s Sanctuary, <sup>42</sup>, <sup>43</sup>, <sup>74</sup>. David’s Prayers for Protection and Deliverance from his Enemies, <sup>3</sup>, <sup>4</sup>, <sup>9</sup>, <sup>55</sup>, <sup>56</sup>, <sup>57</sup>, <sup>58</sup>, <sup>59</sup>, <sup>64</sup>, <sup>70</sup>, <sup>71</sup>, <sup>86</sup>.

<p style="text-align:right;" markdown="1">Humility of Spirit exemplified, <sup>131</sup>.</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">— confidence in God in Danger, <sup>142</sup>.</p>

The Believer’s Prayers for Protection and Deliverance from Wicked Men, <sup>3</sup>, <sup>4</sup>, <sup>5</sup>, <sup>10</sup>,<sup>12</sup>, <sup>13</sup>, <sup>17</sup>, <sup>25</sup>, <sup>26</sup>, <sup>42</sup>, <sup>43</sup>, <sup>54</sup>, <sup>120</sup>, <sup>140</sup>. — Thanksgivings for Deliverance from his Enemies, <sup>11</sup>, <sup>18</sup>, <sup>27</sup>, <sup>31</sup>, <sup>40</sup>, <sup>68</sup>, <sup>138</sup>.

<p style="text-align:right;" markdown="1">Jehovah celebrated as the Guardian of His People, <sup>121</sup>.</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">— Good Resolutions in prospect of being put in possession of the Throne, <sup>101</sup>.</p>

<p style="text-align:right;" markdown="1">The Believer’s Potential Confessions and Deprecations, <sup>6</sup>, <sup>32</sup>, <sup>38</sup>, <sup>39</sup>, <sup>51</sup>, <sup>102</sup>, <sup>130</sup>, <sup>143</sup>.</p>

<!-- PAGE 1045 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">— Exhortations to Trust in God, and to study Godliness from the example of his own deliverance, <sup>31</sup>, <sup>34</sup>.</p>

<p style="text-align:right;" markdown="1">Lamentations of the Believer when under very Severe Afflictions, and almost at the point of despair, <sup>88</sup>.</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">The Blessedness of the Righteous, and the Final Perdition of the ungodly, <sup>1</sup>, <sup>36</sup>, <sup>37</sup>, <sup>52</sup>, <sup>112</sup>, <sup>128</sup>.</p>

<p style="text-align:right;" markdown="1">The Believer’s Thanksgiving for Deliverance from great Danger, <sup>28</sup>, <sup>30</sup>, <sup>61</sup>, <sup>116</sup>.</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">True Believers distinguished from Hypocrites, <sup>15</sup>, <sup>24</sup>.</p>

The supposed Happiness of the Ungodly evanescent; and good men, however much afflicted, the Objects of the Divine Regard, <sup>49</sup>. Reprehension of those who place religion in mere Ritual Observances, and under the veil of these shelter Impurity of Heart and Life, <sup>50</sup>.

<p style="text-align:right;" markdown="1">The Goodness of God to His Ancient People, <sup>77</sup>, <sup>78</sup>, <sup>105</sup>, <sup>114</sup>.</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">The Believer separates himself from idolaters, and rest in God as his Portion, <sup>16</sup>.</p>

<p style="text-align:right;" markdown="1">Prophetic Denunciations against the Enemies of Christ, <sup>109</sup>.</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">The Protection which God affords to His People in the hour of Danger or Calamity, <sup>91</sup>.</p>

<p style="text-align:right;" markdown="1">The Excellence of God’s Word, <sup>19</sup>, <sup>119</sup>.</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">Jehovah celebrated as the Shepherd of His People, <sup>23</sup>.</p>

Celebration of the Glory of God as manifested in Creation, and in the Order and various Wonders of Nature, <sup>19</sup>, <sup>29</sup>, <sup>93</sup>, <sup>104</sup>. The Stubborn and Ungrateful Character of God’s Ancient People, <sup>78</sup>, <sup>95</sup>.

<p style="text-align:right;" markdown="1">The Providence of God on Shipwrecks, Famines, and in all the ills which befall Mankind, as well as in the Happy Issue of Events, <sup>117</sup>.</p>

<!-- PAGE 1046 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">The Jewish Church’s Confession of the Sins of their Fathers, <sup>116</sup>.</p>

<p style="text-align:right;" markdown="1">The Divine Omniscience, <sup>139</sup>.</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">Corrupt State of the Jewish Church brought about by the Reign of Saul <sup>14</sup>, <sup>53</sup>.</p>

<p style="text-align:right;" markdown="1">The Blessing of having Children, <sup>127</sup>.</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">A Common Form of Prayer to be used by the Ancient Church in behalf of the King and Kingdom of Israel when threatened with Danger, <sup>20</sup>.</p>

<p style="text-align:right;" markdown="1">Prayers for Defense and Personal Protection, when Persecuted under False Charges, <sup>7</sup>, <sup>35</sup>.</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">A Public and Solemn Thanksgiving for the Prosperous Condition of the King of Israel, <sup>21</sup>.</p>

<p style="text-align:right;" markdown="1">Prayers that the spirit may be bridled under unprovoked Injuries, <sup>141</sup>.</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">Jerusalem celebrated as the Seat of the Ark and of the Worship of God, <sup>122</sup>.</p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">On Uncharitable Judgments pronounced on the Righteous when severely afflicted by the hand of God, <sup>41</sup>. Prayers for God’s Presence in the Temple Worship, <sup>132</sup>.</p>

<p style="text-align:right;" markdown="1">Kings Addressed and Warned, <sup>2</sup>.</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">Songs of Triumph upon successful War, <sup>60</sup>, <sup>108</sup>.</p>

Character and Doom of Wicked Rulers and Judges, <sup>58</sup>, <sup>82</sup>. Prayers in the Church under Persecution, or in the Seasons of Calamity or of Threatened Danger, <sup>37</sup>, <sup>44</sup>, <sup>74</sup>, <sup>79</sup>, <sup>80</sup>, <sup>83</sup>, <sup>89</sup>, <sup>94</sup>, <sup>115</sup>, <sup>123</sup>, <sup>129</sup>, <sup>144</sup>.

<p style="text-align:right;" markdown="1">Lamentations over the Brevity and Miseries of Human Life, <sup>90</sup>.</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">The Safety of the Church celebrated, <sup>125</sup>.</p>

<p style="text-align:right;" markdown="1">The Believer incites himself to Praise God for the display of His Perfections in the Government of the World, and especially for His Goodness to the Children of Men,</p>

<!-- PAGE 1047 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><sup>145</sup>. Thanksgivings for the Deliverances of the Church, <sup>46</sup>, <sup>48</sup>, <sup>66</sup>, <sup>76</sup>, <sup>124</sup>.</p>

Exhortations to Praise God because of His Justice displayed in the Protection of His People, and in the Destruction of the Wicked, <sup>92</sup>. The Church’s Affliction in Babylon, <sup>137</sup>.

The Prosperity of Abraham in Distinction from the surrounding Nations are incited to Praise God for their peculiar Mercies and Privileges, <sup>95</sup>, <sup>99</sup>, <sup>134</sup>, <sup>135</sup>, <sup>136</sup>, <sup>149</sup>. The Church’s Prayers in Babylon, <sup>102</sup>.

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">They are called upon to Praise Him with every variety of Musical and Privileges, <sup>95</sup>, <sup>99</sup>, <sup>134</sup>, <sup>136</sup>, <sup>149</sup>. Prayer of the returned captives, that God would deliver them from the Calamities with which they were still afflicted, <sup>135</sup>.</p>

<p style="text-align:right;" markdown="1">They are called upon to Praise Him with every variety of Musical Instruments, <sup>140</sup>.</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">Song of Triumph and Thanksgiving of the Returned Captives, <sup>126</sup>.</p>

All Mankind, and not merely the Jews, are exhorted to Praise God, <sup>96</sup>, <sup>98</sup>, <sup>100</sup>, <sup>117</sup>. The Christian Church celebrated, <sup>2</sup>, <sup>45</sup>, <sup>72</sup>, <sup>87</sup>, <sup>97</sup>.

All Creatures, Rational and Irrational, Animate and Inanimate, are invited to Praise Him, <sup>148</sup>. The Triumph of the Church over the Proud Despisers of God, <sup>75</sup>. The Beauty of Christian Concord, <sup>133</sup>. Messiah’s Prayers under his Sufferings, <sup>22</sup>, <sup>69</sup>. Messiah’s Regal and Priestly Offices celebrated,

<!-- PAGE 1049 -->

<p class="title-block-h1" style="text-align:center; font-size:22px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#0000d4">FOOTNOTES</span></p>

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 79</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftc371</span> If this psalm was written on the taking of Jerusalem by Nebuchadnezzar, or during the Babylonish captivity, it would appear, from this verse, that when the Chaldeans destroyed Jerusalem, they left the bodies of the slain unburied, to be devoured by beasts and birds of prey.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc372</span> Similar threatenings are to be found in Isaiah 14:19, 20; Jeremiah 8:2.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc373</span> Street, instead of “our neighbors,” reads, “those that dwell among us;” and has the following note: — “Those foreigners who sojourn among us; <span style="color:#008080">wnynkçl</span>, from <span style="color:#008080">ˆkç</span>, *to inhabit or dwell*; <span style="color:#0000d4">geitosin hJmwn</span>, *our neighbors*, Septuagint. But that rendering does not sufficiently express the distressed and humbled state of Israel, as described in the Hebrew; they were so reduced, that not only neighboring nations, but even those foreigners who sojourned amongst them, had the insolence to deride them, even in their own country.” Dr Adam Clarke explains, *We are become a reproach to our neighbors*, thus: “The Idumeans, Philistines, Phoenicians, Ammonites, and Moabites, all gloried in the subjugation of this people; and their insults to them were mixed with blasphemies against God.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc374</span> “C’est, ire.” — *Fr*. *marg*. “That is, anger.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc375</span> This and the preceding verse are almost exactly the same with Jeremiah 10:25. “Pour out thy fury upon the heathen that know thee not, and upon the families that call not on thy name: for they have eaten up Jacob, and devoured him, and consumed him; and have made his habitation desolate.” From this, some have thought that Jeremiah, who was one of the prophets of the captivity, was the inspired writer of this psalm.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc376</span> “Mettans en avant l’absurdite qui en reviendroit, si Dieu ne punissoit les persecuteurs.” — *Fr*.

<!-- PAGE 1050 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftc377</span> “<span style="color:#008080">rpk</span>, *chapper*, *be propitiated*, or *receive an atonement* (<span style="color:#008080">wnytafh</span> <span style="color:#008080">l[</span>, *al chatoteinu*) *on account of our sins*.” *— Dr Adam Clarke*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc378</span> Horsley, who guesses that this psalm was composed during the distresses of Manasseh’s reign, supposes “the prisoner” to mean Manasseh.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc379</span> “C’est, les condamnez a mort.” — *Fr*. *marg*. “That is, those who are condemned to death.” “*Sons of death*, either those who were condemned to death because of their crimes, or condemned to be destroyed by their oppressors. Both these senses apply to the Israelites: they were sons of death, i.e., worthy of death because of their sins against God. They were condemned to death, or utter destruction, by their Babylonish enemies.” — *Dr Adam Clarke*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc380</span> “*Sevenfold*, i.e., in excessively great measure, — (comp. Genesis 4:15, 24; 1 Samuel 2:5,) *— into their bosom*.

This is an allusion to the custom of folding the loose garment worn by the natives of Eastern countries, so as to make it a recipient of gifts. Comp. Psalm 35:13; Isaiah 65:6; Jeremiah 32:18; Luke 6:38.” — *Cresswell*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc381</span> “C’est, te rendrons graces.” — *Fr*. *marg*. “That is, will give thee thanks.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc382</span> “Car ce n’est pas que les fideles se veuillent yci souler a veoir espandre le sang humain.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc383</span> “Laquelle apparoist quand il fait la vengence des outrages qu’on a faits aux siens.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc384</span> “C’est a dire, de la puissance de Dieu.” — *Fr*.

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 80</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftc385</span> This is the opinion of Hammond, who supposes that this psalm “is a complaint of the troubles of God’s Church and people, probably in time of captivity, or by way of prediction of it.” “Why Ephraim, Benjamin, and Manasseh, and no other, are here named,” says he, “must be learned from the order of the Israelites’ march in the wilderness, Numbers 2. For there, next after the ark, the pledge of

<!-- PAGE 1051 -->

God’s special presence and assistance, did these three tribes follow: ‘Then the tabernacle of the congregation shall set forward,’ etc., verse 17; ‘On the west side (i.e., next behind it) shall be the standard of the camp of Ephraim,’ verse 18; ‘and his host,’ etc., verse 19. ‘And by him shall be the tribe of Manasseh,’ verse 20; ‘and his host,’ etc., verse 21. ‘Then the tribe of Benjamin, and his host,’ verses 22, 23. Now the returning from the captivity, the desire whereof is the business of this psalm, being a parallel to the delivery from Egypt, God’s ‘leading them back, stirring up himself, and coming to save them,’ is very fitly. begged, and described in a style resembling the former rescue.” Merrick accounts for Ephraim, Benjamin, and Manasseh, being particularly specified, by supposing the psalm to have been written at a time when some enemy was advancing towards these tribes, which were contiguously situated, or was directing his march to Jerusalem, through their territories. “Such an occasion,” he observes, “might make it very proper for the Psalmist to pray that the people of those tribes might particularly be made spectators of the divine interposition. If the psalm was not written on any such occasion, it may be most reasonable to suppose, that Benjamin, Joseph’s only brother by the same mother, and Ephraim and Manasseh his sons, are in the second verse equivalent to Joseph; who, in the preceding verse, represents the whole posterity of Israel.”

<!-- PAGE 1052 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftc386</span> The argument which Calvin here adduces in support of the opinion, that this psalm relates to the ten tribes which constituted the kingdom of Israel, in contradistinction to the kingdom of Judah, is evidently inconclusive. He seems to have forgotten the fact that the tribe of Benjamin, which is expressly specified, did not belong to the kingdom of Israel, but formed a part of the kingdom of Judah, — a fact which is altogether destructive of the argument by which he attempts to prove that the psalm relates exclusively or especially to the kingdom of Israel. The whole of God’s ancient people seem therefore to be intended. It may farther be observed, that the calamities which are referred to are so extensive and general, as to render it in a high degree probable, that the entire body of that people are spoken of. This view is also confirmed, from the introduction of the similitude of a vine transplanted from Egypt. The subject of the psalm may be the same with that of the 79th — the calamitous condition into which the chosen people were brought by the arms of Nebuchadnezzar.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc387</span> The original word for “stir up” is , <span style="color:#008080">hrrw[</span>, *orera*, from <span style="color:#008080">rw[</span>, *ur*, *was excited*. “This word,” says Dimock, “seems to convey the idea of God’s having been *asleep* during the Babylonish captivity. See Isaiah 51:9.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc388</span> Literally, “wilt thou smoke (with wrath;”) i.e., be very angry. — See Psalm 74:1.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc389</span> “There cannot,” says Bishop Horne, “be a more striking picture of Zion in captivity! Her bread is dipped in tears; and her cup is filled to the brim with them: no time is free from grief and lamentation!”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc390</span> The LXX. read this verse as follows. <span style="color:#0000d4">Εκάλυψενόρη ἡ σκιὰ αὐτῆς,</span> <span style="color:#0000d4">καὶ αί ἀναδενδράδες αὐτῆς τὰς κέδρους tou Θεοῦ</span> . “The shadow thereof covered the hills, and the branches thereof [χοςερεδ the cedars of God.” The LXX. seem to have read <span style="color:#008080">hsk</span>, *casah*, *covered*, instead of <span style="color:#008080">wsk</span>, *cossu*, *were covered*. With this agree the versions of the Syriac, Arabic, and Vulgate; and this is the reading adopted by Hare, Houbigant, Lowth, and Horsley. “Is it an extravagant image of a flourishing vine,” says Lowth, “to say, that it climbed up even the highest cedars, spread itself along the branches, and covered the very top of them?” “The image,” says Merrick, “may, I think, well be allowed in the description of an allegorical vine, which is represented as stretching out her branches unto the sea, and her boughs unto the river; especially when compared with what Kaempfer says of some foreign vines. ‘Maximum proventum vites tribuunt, quae nulla jutae cultura palmites per summa spargunt fastigia arborum.’ — *Amoenitat*. *Exot*. *Fascic*. 2, *Relat*. 9, § 2, page 390. The author of the History of the Piratical States of Barbary (published in 1750) informs us that some of the vines near Algiers ‘climb to the tops of very lofty trees, and, extending themselves to others, form natural bowers,’ page 163. And Beverley, in his History of Virginia, (page 116, ed. 2d,) affirms that he has seen great trees *covered* with single vines, and those vines almost hidden with grapes. . . . The vine’s covering the cedars, in the Psalmist’s description, might be intended to suggest an idea not only of its extent, but also of its sovereignty, (agreeably to what

<!-- PAGE 1053 -->

Musculus writes on the place: ‘*Operti fuerunt montes umbra ejus*, *et ramis ejus cedri Dei*: Ponit haec de potentia regni Israelitici,’ etc.,) as a Greek poet has, from this very circumstance, represented the vine as the mistress of the trees. (Nonnus, *Dionysiac*. L. 12, 278, 279.”)

<span style="color:#800000">ftc391</span> *The sea — the river — i*.e., the Mediterranean, which was the Western, and the Euphrates, which was the Eastern boundary of Palestine. The Divine promise respecting the extent of the territory of the chosen people runs in these terms, (Deuteronomy 11:24,) “From the river Euphrates to the uttermost sea shall your coast be.” And it was fulfilled in the days of Solomon, (1 Kings 4:21; Psalm 72:8.) In his time there were Hebrew colonies and garrisons near the river Euphrates.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc392</span> According to the Talmud, the middle letter of the word rendered *forest* in this verse, is the middle letter of the Hebrew Psalter.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc393</span> *The boar out of the forest hath wasted it*. “This terrible animal is both fierce and cruel, and so swift, that few of the savage tribes can outstrip him in running. His chief abode, says Forbes, is in the forests and jungles; but, when the grain is nearly ripe, he commits great ravages in the fields and sugar plantations. That ferocious and destructive animal, not satisfied with devouring the fruit, lacerates and breaks with his sharp and powerful tusks the branches of the vine, or, with his snout, digs it up by the roots, pollutes it with his touch, or tramples it under his feet.” — (*Paxton*’*s Illustrations*, volume 2, page 66.) Homer complains of the ravages of this animal, (Iliad, 9. 535;) and Mr Ward remarks, that the buffaloes and wild hogs make the like ravages in the orchards of the Hindoos; to prevent which, men are placed day and night in proper situations to guard against them. — (*Ward*’*s Hindoos*, volume 2, page 327.)

<span style="color:#800000">ftc394</span> “Mais du travail qu’il avoit prins a la provigner.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc395</span> “<span style="color:#008080">hnmsrky</span>, (*jechar-semenna*,) *will destroy it*. *Targum*, *Will tear it up with its tusk*. *Fut*. *pih*. From <span style="color:#008080">µsrj</span>, *he cut off*, *cut down*, *consumed*, a quadriliteral, same as the Chaldaic <span style="color:#008080">µswq</span>. Occurs here only in Scripture, and, according to others, is compounded of <span style="color:#008080">çrk</span>, *a belly*, as though <span style="color:#008080">çrkw</span>, *will fill the belly from it*.” — *Bythner*.

<!-- PAGE 1054 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftc396</span> “Hammond thinks it most probable that <span style="color:#008080">l[</span>, *al*, *upon*, is an expletive, or that it may refer to <span style="color:#008080">har</span>, *reeh*, *behold* or *look*, the last verb except one in the preceding verse, <span style="color:#008080">l[ har</span>, *reeh al*, *look upon*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc397</span> The original word which Calvin renders *branch* is <span style="color:#008080">ˆb</span>, *ben*, *son*. “Where,” says Horsley, “does <span style="color:#008080">ˆb</span> signify a branch?” It is; however, so used in Genesis 49:22, where it is said, “Joseph is a fruitful <span style="color:#008080">ˆb</span>, *ben*, *bough*, or *branch*, by a well.” The reading of some MSS., and of the Septuagint, Vulgate, Syriac, Æthiopic, and Arabic versions, is *the son of man*, as in the 17th verse; and eighteen of Kennicott’s and De Rossi’s MSS. read <span style="color:#008080">µda ˆb</span>, *ben adam*, *son of man*. It has been thought by many that Christ is here intended. Aben Ezra and R. Obadiah thus interpret the passage. The Chaldee paraphrase is, “And upon the King Messiah whom thou hast strengthened for thyself.” Hare, Green, Horsley, and Morison, consider the last clause of this verse, “and the branch which thou hast strengthened for thyself,” as a misplaced anticipation of the latter clause of the 17th verse.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc398</span> Horsley thinks that the word <span style="color:#008080">hjwsk</span>, *kesuchah*, which Calvin renders as a verb, “it is cut down,” is probably the noun <span style="color:#008080">hjws</span>, with the comparative <span style="color:#008080">k</span>, *caph*, prefixed — “It is consumed in fire like refuse;” and he refers to Parkhurst’s Lexicon, under the roots, <span style="color:#008080">jsk</span>, and <span style="color:#008080">hjs</span>. “This verse,” says he, “with the two preceding, should be thus rendered: —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">‘Return, we beseech thee, O God of Hosts!</span> <span style="color:#000080">Look down from heaven and behold, And visit this vine;</span> <span style="color:#000080">Even the plant which thine own right hand planted,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Burnt with fire like refuse.—</span> <span style="color:#000080">At the rebuke of thy countenance they shall perish,’</span></p>

“ — *they shall perish*: *They*, the spoilers of the vineyard, described under the image of the wild boar and beast in the 13th verse.” “The Bishop’s reading of verse 16,” says Dr Morison, “is very satisfactory.” “*They perish*. This should either be rendered as by our translators and Mr Ainsworth, and then the words refer to the vine of the Jewish Church; but if in the future, as by Bishop Horsley, it must refer to their

<!-- PAGE 1055 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">heathen persecutors. Bishop Horne mentions both, and the original will admit of either.” — *Williams*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftc399</span> “Surely ,<span style="color:#008080">hnk</span>, should not be translated *vineyard*, but *plant*: and probably <span style="color:#008080">w</span> should be translated, or understood to mean, *even*. See Noldius, *Sign*. 38.” *— Arcbishop Secker*. “Michaelis and Gesenius derive it from <span style="color:#008080">ˆnk</span>, *texit*, with the suffix <span style="color:#008080">h</span>. Bochart considers it an Egyptian word. ‘<span style="color:#008080">hnk</span>, verto *plantam* ex sententia Bocharti (in Phaleg. lib. 1, cap. 15 and 16, edit. Leusd.) qui putat vocem esse Ægyptiacam. Nam, auctore Plutarcho in Iside, hederam Ægyptii <span style="color:#0000d4">χενοίριν</span>, h. e. <span style="color:#0000d4">φυτὸν Osiridov</span>, *plantam* Osiridis vocabant.’ Dathe. De Rossi concurs.” — *Rogers*’ *Book of Psalms*, *etc*., volume 2, 231.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc400</span> Under the same allegorical imagery the Prophet Ezekiel represents the afflicted state of his country, (Ezekiel 19:10, 12, 13.)

<span style="color:#800000">ftc400</span> Muis, Walford, and others, in like manner, suppose these titles, *The Man of thy right hand*, and *The Son of man*, to belong to the people of Israel. Walford translates the 15th and 17th verses thus: —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">“The scion, which thy right hand planted;</span> <span style="color:#000080">Even the branch, which thou madest strong for thyself.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Let thy support be extended to the Man of thy right hand;</span> <span style="color:#000080">To the Son of man, whom thou madest strong for thyself.”</span></p>

And he observes on the 17th verse, “The Psalmist here quits the figurative representation, and speaks literally of the people of Israel, whom God had chosen, and so greatly favored.” “From comparing 2 Chronicles 36:22, 23; Isaiah 44:26-28; 45:1-11, and Jeremiah 25:12, 13,” says Dimock, “with this verse, might not

<!-- PAGE 1056 -->
Jeremiah, or whoever was the author of this psalm, mean *Cyrus*, by these titles, who was prophesied of as the restorer of Israel, by *name*, above a hundred years before his birth?” It has been thought by others, and it is highly probable, that the phraseology here employed contains a mystic allusion to the Messiah. The pious Israelites were accustomed, in times of great calamity, to look forward with longing desire to the days of Him who should reign over the house of Jacob for ever, and of whose kingdom there should be no end. These striking expressions, *The Man of thy right hand*, and *The Son of man*, apply in the fullest and most perfect sense to Christ. If *the Man of God*’*s right hand* be the man placed there, to whom can the title apply but to him? for, “to which of the angels said God at any time, Sit on my right hand?” (Hebrews 1:3;) and much less has he said this of any Jewish king. As to the other appellation, *The Son of man*, it is one of Christ’s most definite titles, being given to him in Scripture no less than seventy-one times; in sixty-seven instances by himself; once by Daniel; once by the martyr Stephen; and twice by the Apostle John in the Revelation. He it is, too, whom the Father has made strong for the salvation of his Church, and who will yet turn away iniquity from the chosen people, and restore them to a place in the Church, so that henceforth they “will not go back from God.”

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 81</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1057 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftc401</span> There are various opinions as to the time and occasion of the composition of this psalm. Bishop Horsley observes, “It is certainly older than the time of David; for the use of Joseph’s name, in the 5th verse, as the name of the whole nation, shows that it was composed before Judah became the principal tribe, while the place of worship was in the tribe of Ephraim; that is, among Joseph’s descendants.” “This, however,” says Fry, “is not conclusive, as a psalm, whenever composed, referring to the events of those times, might use the same distinctions.” According to Walford, it “was most likely written to be sung at some celebration of the feast of the Passover, during the reign of Jehoshaphat or of Hezekiah.” But the generally received opinion is, that it was composed, in the first instance, for the feast of trumpets. This feast was celebrated on the first day of the month *Tisri*, which was the beginning of the Jewish year, answering to our September. It has been supposed by some, that this feast was appointed in commemoration of the creation of the world, which is conjectured to have been completed at that season of the year. The Hebrew months were lunar, and the first day of each month had its religious services, accompanied with sound of trumpets, Numbers 10:10; but the feast of trumpets was kept with additional sacrifices, Leviticus 23:24; Numbers 29:1. The trumpets were blown from sunrise until sunset. It appears from the book of the Jewish Liturgy, that this psalm is still sung at that feast. “It may have been used,” observes Dr Adam Clarke, “in celebrating the feast of trumpets on the first of Tisri; the feast of tabernacles, on the fifteenth of the same month; the creation of the world; the feast of the new moons; and the deliverance of the Israelites from Egypt; to all which circumstances it appears to refer.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc402</span> “*Take a psalm*. Ainsworth, *Take up a psalm*. Bishop Horsley says, ‘The word (psalm) must in this place denote some musical instrument.’ But, with all due deference to his Lordship, suppose a clergyman in the present day were to say to his clerk, ‘Strike up a psalm!’ (quite a similar phrase,) would the clerk understand him to mean a musical instrument? Certainly not.” — *Williams*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc403</span> For an account of these musical instruments, see Appendix.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc404</span> Hammond translates this verse thus, “Blow the trumpet on the first day of the month, on the new moon, on the day of our feast.” “The word <span style="color:#008080">çdjb</span>,” says he, “must here be rendered, *in the beginning of the month*, that so <span style="color:#008080">hskb</span>, that follows, may be rendered, as it truly signifies, *in the new moon*. It is true, that from <span style="color:#008080">çdj</span>, *new*, <span style="color:#008080">çdj</span> indifferently signifies the *novilunium*, and *the first day of the month*; but here, *the new moon* being peculiarly expressed by <span style="color:#008080">hsk</span>, to avoid tautology,<span style="color:#008080">çdj</span> must be rendered *the new month*; i.e., *the first day of the month*. The Syriac sets this down here most expressly, ‘In the beginning or first of the month, and in the new moon;’ which, meeting always together, were festival among the Jews, and so the trumpet was to be sounded thereon.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc407</span> The word translated *pot* was, according to Kennicott, a large vessel in which the earth was mixed and worked up for making the bricks. The LXX. the Vulgate, Symmachus, Jerome, Street, Parkhurst, Ainsworth, Fry, Walford, and others, render the original word, by *the basket*. Parkhurst observes, that baskets might probably be employed both in carrying the earth of which the bricks were made, and also the bricks themselves.

<!-- PAGE 1058 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftc408</span> “*When he went forth*, etc.; i.e., When God went forth to destroy the first-born in all the land of Egypt, on account of which the passover was appointed.” — *Walford*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc409</span> “Going forth (<span style="color:#008080">l[</span>) over the land of Egypt seems to express dominion over it, which God exercised in bringing out the Israelites; and they were then in what may be called a state of superiority over the Egyptians, and went out with a high hand. Exodus 14:8; Numbers 33:3. And soon after that the law was given.” —

*Archbishop Secker*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc410</span> The Septuagint, Syriac, Vulgate, and all the versions except the Chaldee, have the third person, “*He* heard a language which *he* understood not;” Doederlein reads, “I heard a voice which I understood not;” and retaining the first person, interprets the words as an abrupt exclamation of the Psalmist upon feeling himself suddenly influenced by a divine afflatus, and upon hearing an oracle addressed to him by God, which consisted of what immediately follows, from the 6th verse to the close of the psalm, and which is spoken in the person of God. This voice he heard, but he did not understand it; that is, he did not fully comprehend its design and import.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc411</span> “The Egyptian language was not intelligible to the children of Jacob; for Joseph spake to his brethren by an interpreter, when he appeared as ruler of Egypt, and did not as yet choose to make himself known to them. See Genesis 42:23.” — *Street*.

<!-- PAGE 1059 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftc412</span> Bishop Lowth understands by “the secret place of thunder” the communication of the Israelites with God upon mount Sinai, the awfulness of which is expressed by these few words. (Lowth’s Lectures on the Sacred Poetry of the Hebrews, volume 2, page 220.) Walford reads, “I answered thee by thunder, from a hidden retreat;” and he observes, that this contains “a reference to the majestic display on Sinai, where, though the symbols of the present Deity were seen and heard, the lightnings and thunders, he himself was concealed from all human view.” The only objection which can be made against interpreting this of Sinai is, that the murmuring at Meribah, Exodus 17, was before the thundering on Sinai, Exodus 19; whereas here the thunder is mentioned first, and then what took place at Meribah in the end of the verse. But this objection is easily removed; for in the poetical compositions of Scripture strict order is not, always observed in the narration of facts. Thus in Psalm 83:9, the victory over the Midianites (Judges 7) is mentioned before that over Sisera, (Judges 4,) which was the victory first achieved.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc413</span> Literally “the waters of contradiction;” <span style="color:#008080">hbyrm</span>, *meribah*, from <span style="color:#008080">bwr</span>, *rub*, *to quarrel*, being a noun signifying *contention*, *strife*. It is therefore fitly used as the name of the place in the desert where the Israelites quarrelled with Moses. “The local specification,” observes Bishop Mant, “as used in our Bible translation, is much more poetical than the rendering in the Common Prayer-Book, ‘the waters of strife.’” “The mention of Meribah,” says Lowth, “introduces another idea, namely, the ingratitude and contumacy of the Israelites, who appear to have been ever unmindful of the favors and indulgence of their heavenly Benefactor.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc414</span> Street reads, “and I will make a testimony with thee.” “<span style="color:#008080">hdy[a</span>” says he, “is in the hiphil conjugation, which frequently signifies to make or cause a thing to be made. The ark is called *the ark of the testimony*, <span style="color:#008080">td[h ˆwra</span>, Exodus 30:26, and *the ark of the covenant*, Joshua 3:6, and Exodus 25:21. Moses is commanded to put *the testimony* which God shall give him into the ark. It is plain, therefore, that the covenant and the testimony are the same.” “I *will testify unto thee*. I will, upon all occasions, give the oracular direction, so that thou shalt have no occasion to resort to other gods, nor shall any pretended god have power to harm thee.” — *Horsley*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc415</span> “Ou, perversite, ou, durete.” — *Fr*. *marg*. “Or, the perversity, or, the hardness.” Hammond reads: “I gave them up unto the imaginations of their hearts.” Horsley: “So I gave them up to the government of their own hearts.” Fry: “And I gave them up to the desires of their heart.” Walford: “Therefore I gave them up to the purposes of their heart.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc416</span> “Heathen, or foreign god.” — *Hammond*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc417</span> In our English Bible it is, “*He* should have fed them.” The LXX., Vulgate, and Syriac versions, Green, Walford, and others, read as Calvin does, “I would have fed them.” “This is the preferable reading,” says Walford, “as the common lection introduces a too sudden change of person.”

<!-- PAGE 1060 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftc418</span> “Nothing,” says Dr Adam Clarke on this verse, “can be more plaintive than the original: *sense* and *sound* are surprisingly united. I scruple not to say to him who understands the Hebrew, however learned, he has never found in any poet, Greek or Latin, a finer example of deep-seated grief, unable to express itself in appropriate words, without frequent interruptions of sighs and sobs, terminated with a mournful cry — <span style="color:#008080">yl [mç ym[ wl</span> <span style="color:#008080">wklhy ykrdb larçy</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Loo-ghammee-shomeagh-lee*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Yishrael-bid’ rakee-yehallekoo!*</span>

“He who can give the proper guttural pronunciation to the letter <span style="color:#008080">[</span>, *ayin*; and gives the <span style="color:#008080">w</span>, *vau*, and the <span style="color:#008080">y</span>, *yod*, their full Asiatic sound; and does not pinch them to death by a compressed and worthless European enunciation; will at once be convinced of the propriety of this remark.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc419</span> See volume 1, page 301.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc420</span> “*Their time*, etc.: that is, the time, the continuance, the prosperity of my people, would have been durable.” — *Warner*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc421</span> It is an usual phrase with the Hebrews to call the most esteemed part of anything <span style="color:#008080">blj</span>, *cheleb*, “the fat.” The word is used with this combination in Deuteronomy 32:14; and is adopted again in <span style="color:#800000"><19E714></span>Psalm 147:14. See also Genesis 45:18; Numbers 18:29; and Psalm 73:4. The translators of our English version have rendered it here “the finest of the wheat.”

<!-- PAGE 1061 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftc422</span> Palestine abounded in wild bees, which, living in the crevices of rocks, and in the hollows of trees, furnished honey in great plenty. To this there are frequent allusions in Scripture. In Deuteronomy 32:13, Moses, speaking of God’s goodness to Israel in the song with which he closed his long and eventful career, says, “He made him suck honey out of the rock.” As an evidence of the great abundance of wild honey in that country, we may refer to 1 Samuel 14:25, where it is said, “And all they of the land came to a wood, and there was honey upon the ground; and when the people were come to the wood, behold the honey dropped.” In proof of the same point, reference may be also made to the fact, that a part of the food of John the Baptist in the wilderness was wild honey, which most probably he found in rocks or hollow trees. In Scripture, the country is frequently described by a familiar phrase, as “A land flowing with milk and honey;” and in Job 20:17, we meet with the strong expression of “Brooks, floods, and rivers of honey.” Palestine is still remarkable for this natural production. It may be observed, that the change of person in this last verse from the third to the first is highly poetical.

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 82</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftc423</span> Kimchi thinks it probable that this psalm was written in the days of Jehoshaphat, and refers to 2 Chronicles 19:5-7, as indicating the time and occasion of its composition. We are there informed that Jehoshaphat; “set judges in the land, throughout all the fenced cities of Judah, city by city;” and in instructing them with regard to their duty, he uses nearly the same words as those in the beginning of this psalm. Dr Morison takes a different view. “This psalm,” says he, “was composed in all probability in the days of Hezekiah, in reference to certain wicked magistrates, (2 Chronicles 29:30,) who had grievously perverted the administration of justice, who were guilty of great oppression, and who had done much to introduce a state of general national corruption. King Jehoshaphat had reformed several public abuses which had crept into the judicatories of Israel in his time, (2 Chronicles 19:7,) but before the reign of Hezekiah, things had reverted to their former unhappy condition; so that a public national reformation was loudly demanded. The psalm contains an exhortation to the judges of Israel, and a reproof for their negligence and oppression.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc424</span> “Ou, il jugeras au milieu les dieux.” — *Fr*. *marg*. “Or, he will judge in the midst the gods.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc425</span> “C’est, faites justice.” — *Fr*. *marg*. “That is, do justice to.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc426</span> Horsley translates the first verse thus: —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">“God standeth in the assembly;</span> <span style="color:#000080">God, in the midst of the gods, giveth sentence.”</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1062 -->

On which he has the following note: — “In what assembly? The assembly of his holy angels. The Psalmist, I think, poetically imagines the celestial court assembled for the business of this review of the proceedings of the earth’s judges, and God, in the midst of his angels, taxing their iniquity, and awarding their punishment.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc427</span> “The Psalmist having thus far addressed himself to the administrators of justice, as if wearied with his ineffectual remonstrance, here suddenly turns away and condemns their inattention and perverseness. The change of person is a natural indication of the earnestness of the speaker, and has a lively effect.” *— Mant*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc428</span> “*All the foundations of the earth*, etc. Rather, *of the land*; that is, truth and justice, the foundation of all good government, and the only security of a state, are now altogether violated or disregarded.” — *Warner*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc429</span> “*Ye are all the children of the Most High*, an Hebrew idiom, signifying men of the highest rank and power. Comp. Psalm 29:1; 89:7.” — *Cresswell*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc430</span> This is the reading in our English Bible, on which Archbishop Secker remarks, “It seems needless to say that these princes shall fall like one of the princes.” He thinks with Bishop Hare that the true reading is not <span style="color:#008080">µyrçh</span>, *hassarim*, *the princes*, as in our present copies, but <span style="color:#008080">µyçrh</span>, *harsaim*, *the poor*. The translation, however, given by Calvin, who takes <span style="color:#008080">µyrçh</span> in the vocative case, O *ye princes*! and who, after the word <span style="color:#008080">djak</span>, *cheachad*, for *as one of*, supplies *the people*, makes any alteration of the text unnecessary. Gataker also considers <span style="color:#008080">µyrçh</span>, to be in the vocative case, which is approved by Horsley, Berlin, and others. Dathe takes <span style="color:#008080">µyrçh</span> in the sense of *tyrants*, but brings no authority to prove that the word has this sense. Le Clerc, in the latter part of the verse, after *like one of*, supplies *the many*, reading, “And fall, O ye princes! like one of the many.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc431</span> This is the translation given of these lines in the French version.

<!-- PAGE 1063 -->

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 83</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftc432</span> Compare the 6th, 7th, and 8th verses of the psalm with 2 Chronicles 20:1, 10, 22; and the 12th verse of the psalm with the 11th verse of that chapter.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc433</span> The name of this Levite was Jahaziel, and he is expressly said to be a prophet of the race of Asaph, 2 Chronicles 20:14. It is not unlikely that he is the same with Asaph, the author of this psalm.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc434</span> The Hebrew word translated *thy hidden ones*, primarily means a *treasure*, and is so taken in Psalm 17:14. Accordingly, it is here rendered by Mudge, and French and Skinner, “thy treasured ones :” that is, thy peculiar people: those whom thou hast hitherto protected and kept in perfect safety, as in a place of security and secrecy. The Septuagint reads, <span style="color:#0000d4">κατὰ τῶν ἁγίων sou</span>, “against thy saints.” The word is also sometimes put for the sanctuary, as in Ezekiel 7:22. Some therefore think that the temple, and the treasures contained in it, are intended.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc435</span> “Ils sentiront a la fin a leur grande honte, qu’ils estoyent desnuer de toute vertu.” — *Fr*. “Will at length find, to their great shame, that they were destitute of all power.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc436</span> The Hebrew is <span style="color:#008080">wtrky tyrb</span>, *berith yichrothu*, “they have cut a covenant.” The verb is from <span style="color:#008080">trk</span>, *carath*, *he cut*, which, with the noun, <span style="color:#008080">tyrb</span>, *berith*, signifies *to strike a league*, or *to covenant*. The phrase owes its origin to the custom which prevailed, in ancient times, of sacrificing an animal at the forming of solemn leagues, and dividing the victim in twain, the contracting parties passing between the two pieces; see volume 2, page 264, note. It is then affirmed of these combined enemies of the Jews, that *they had cut the covenant sacrifice*; that they had slain a sacrificial victim, divided it in twain, and passed between the pieces’ thus mutually binding themselves to accomplish their hostile purpose.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc437</span> That is, the Edomites, the descendants of Esau, (Genesis 25:30.) They were a pastoral people, and made great use of tents.

<!-- PAGE 1064 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftc438</span> The Ishmaelites were the descendants of Ishmael, Abraham’s son, by Hagar the Egyptian, (Genesis 25:12-18.) They inhabited part of Arabia.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc439</span> That is, the Moabites, the descendants of Moab, a son of Lot, by one of his daughters, (Genesis 19:37.)

<span style="color:#800000">ftc440</span> The Hagarenes or Hagarites were the posterity of Abraham by Keturah, (who is supposed to have been Hagar,) whom he married after Sarah’s death. They dwelt on the east of Gilead, in the vicinity of the Euphrates. , In the days of Saul war was made upon them by the Reubenites, who, after having nearly destroyed them and expelled them from their country, dwelt in their tents, (1 Chronicles 5:10.) They seem again to have recruited their strength; but where they afterwards dwelt is not known. “They are probably the same,” says Cresswell, “as the Saracens.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc441</span> Gebal, which signifies a mountain, denotes, according to some, the Giblites, who inhabited a district on the Phoenician coast in the neighborhood of Tyre. They were a tribe of the Aborigines of Canaan, and are mentioned as left by Joshua to be conquered after his death, (Joshua 13:5.) They were of considerable service to Hiram, king of Tyre, in preparing materials for Solomon’s temple, as we learn from 1 Kings 5:18, where the original word for *stone-squarers* is <span style="color:#008080">µylbgh</span> *haggibelim*, *the Giblites*; and it would seem from Ezekiel’s speaking of “the ancients of Gebal and the wise men thereof,” (Ezekiel 27:9,) that they rose to no small degree of eminence. The ruins of an ancient city called by the natives Gibyle, situated upon the Mediterranean Sea between Tripoli and Sidon, are supposed to be those of the chief city of the Giblites. If so, these ruins attest its ancient grandeur to have been considerable. Others suppose that Gebal (the Gebalene of the Romans) was a mountainous district inhabited by the Edomites, and extending from the Dead Sea southward to Selal or Petra. By the Arabs it is called Djebal.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc442</span> That is, the Ammonites, the descendants of Ammon, another of Lot’s sons, by one of his daughters, (Genesis 19:38.) They dwelt in Arabia Petrea.

<!-- PAGE 1065 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftc443</span> The Amalekites were a powerful people, who dwelt also in Arabia Petrea, between the Dead Sea and the Red Sea, or between Havilah and Shur, (1 Samuel 15:7,) south of Idumea, and east of the northern part of the Red Sea.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc444</span> The Midianites derive their name from Midian, the son of Abraham, by Keturah, Genesis 25:2. The history here referred to is the complete defeat of that people by Gideon, Judges 7:21, 23.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc445</span> *Kishon* is a torrent which flows from mount Tabor into the sea.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc446</span> *Oreb and Zeeb* were two chiefs or generals of the Midianites, and were slain by the men of Ephraim in their pursuit of the Midianites, Judges 7:24, 25.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc447</span> *Zebah and Zalmunna* were kings of Midian, whom Gideon, after having defeated their army, took prisoners and put to death, Judges 8:10-21.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc448</span> Endor is not mentioned in the account given of the discomfiture of Jabin’s host, and the slaughter of Sisera, in Judges 4; but it appears from Joshua 17:11, which Calvin quotes, to have been a part of the portion which fell to the tribe of Manasseh. In that passage, Taanach and Megiddo are mentioned as districts adjoining to Endor. And in the song of Deborah, the kings of Canaan who fought on the occasion referred to, against the Israelites, are said to have fought “in Taanach by the waters of Megiddo,” Judges 5:19. This may explain why they are said to have perished at Endor, which was near the place where Sisera’s army were destroyed.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc449</span> “*Globum*,” — *Lat*. “*Une boule*,” — *Fr*. The word <span style="color:#008080">lglg</span>, *galeggal*, thus translated, is interpreted by Lowth, “any light thing *whirled* by the wind, chaff, thistle-down, etc.” “<span style="color:#008080">lglg</span> seems here,” says Archbishop Secker, “especially on comparing Isaiah 17:13, to be not a wheel, but some light matter, which the wind whirls round and blows away; *chaff*.” In that passage of Isaiah, where the same Hebrew word occurs, the rendering in our English Bible is “a rolling thing;” and the marginal reading, “thistle-down.” This verse affords a striking exhibition of the nothingness of combined nations before the Almighty. He can make them “like the thistle-down; like the stubble before the wind.”

<!-- PAGE 1066 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftc450</span> The allusion in this verse is to the fires, either accidental or designed, which frequently occur in hot and wooded countries, and which spread to a vast extent, devouring all before them, and continuing their ravages for a long time. Many Eastern and African travelers describe these formidable and alarming fires from personal observation; and such descriptions serve to give a more adequate idea than would otherwise offer itself to an European mind of the Psalmist’s meaning. This language is an expressive image for wide and quick destruction.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc451</span> “*Kindleth the mountains*, that is, the produce of the mountains, trees, plants, etc.” — *Walford*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc452</span> “*Pursue them with thy tempest*, is an evident reference *to the dissipation of the chaff*, and what follows relates clearly to *the expansion of the flame*.” — Note of Henley, in *Lowth*’*s Sacred Poetry of the Hebrews*, volume 1, page 277.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc453</span> “The construction of the words in the close of the psalm lies most probably thus, <span style="color:#008080">w[dyw</span>, *and they shall know*, i.e., *it shall be known* by this means, <span style="color:#008080">hwhy ˚mç hta yk</span>, *thou art thy name Jehovah*, i.e., *that thou art what thy name Jehovah imports*; and what that is, is expounded in the remainder of the verse, <span style="color:#008080">ˆwyl[ ˚dbl</span>, *thou only the Most High over all the earth*; that being indeed the meaning of *Jehovah*, the infinite, eternal, and so the only supreme power over all the world.”. — *Hammond*.

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 84</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftc454</span> “Mais au contraire que par dessus tous empeschemens ils poursuyvront constamment a chercher Dieu, et par maniere de dire, se feront voye la ou il n’y en a point.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc455</span> “Il tesmoigne derechef qu’il estime plus de jouyer de ceste liberte d’assister avec les autres au tabernacle de Dieu, quand mesme il ne devroit vivre qu’un jour, etc.” — *Fr*. “He again testifies, that to enjoy the liberty of assisting with others at the tabernacle of God for only one day was, in his estimation, more to be prized, etc.”

<!-- PAGE 1067 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftc456</span> “It is admitted that the Hebrew preposition here used (*lamed*) may be translated either *by*, *to*, or *for*. When applied to an individual, we consider it as marking the author by whom it was written, or the musician to whose care it was addressed, for adapting it to music. But when addressed to a company of choristers, as the sons of Korah, there seems no doubt but it was intended for them to sing it.” — *Williams*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc457</span> The sons of Korah were the descendants of Korah, whom the earth swallowed up for striving against Moses and against the Lord. In the narrative of that event, we are informed that “the children of Korah died not,” (Numbers 26:10.) They joined not with their father in his sedition, and therefore escaped his punishment. It appears from 1 Chronicles 9:19, and 26:1-19, that their posterity were employed as porters or keepers of the tabernacle and temple. They had also a place among the singers of the temple, (2 Chronicles 20:19.) Their name occurs in the title of nine psalms.

<span style="color:#800000">ftc458</span> “Or est-il, que depuis ce temps-la, il ne perdit jamais la liberte de pouvoir comparoistre devant l’Arche avec les autres, si non une fois et pour bien peu de temps, c’est ascavoir quand il s’enfuit pour la persecution que luy faisoit son fils.” — *Fr*.

<!-- PAGE 1068 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftc459</span> Bochart supposes <span style="color:#008080">rwrd</span>, to signify not the *swallow*, but some kind of wild *dove*; as he observes, that the Æthiopic version renders it the *ring-dove*, and the Septuagint, Vulgate, Chaldee, Syriac, and other ancient versions, the *turtle*. These last probably render it *turtle* from the resemblance of the name to <span style="color:#008080">rwt</span>, *tur*, the common name of that bird. Merrick, in his version, translated it at first *turtle*, but afterwards substituted the more comprehensive name of *dove* instead of turtle, at the suggestion of Dr Lowth. “You have very good authorities for the *turtle*,” says that learned Prelate: “my objection may be merely an English one. The bird which we know by that name is of all others the most retired and shyest; and hardly ever approaches any building, much less makes her nest in any frequented place. Does not this consideration render it an unfit image for the Psalmist’s purpose here? The *dove*, which is only a more general name for the same bird, would not be liable to this objection.” But to remove that difficulty relating to the turtle, Merrick quotes a passage from Sir H. Blunt’s Voyage to the Levant, (page 186, ed. 5) in which that traveler says, that in Turkey, all birds are so tame from not being used to violence, that he had thrown his coat upon *turtle-doves* in the highway. “The Hebrew interpreters,” says the Illustrated Commentary upon the Bible, “believe it is the swallow, and are followed by our version. The word means *freedom*, *deliverance*, and may be supposed to refer to the free manner in which the swallow flies. It is only mentioned again, at least by this name, in Proverbs 26:2; and is there also associated with the *tsippor*, which our version there renders *bird*, instead of *sparrow*. In both texts, the meaning agrees better with the swallow than the turtle-dove.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftc460</span> “Comme estort le pavillon de l’Arche.” — *Fr*.

<!-- PAGE 1069 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftc461</span> This is the sense given in our English Bible; to the accuracy of which Dr Adam Clarke objects. “It is very unlikely,” says he, “that sparrows and swallows, or birds of any kind, should be permitted to build their nests, and hatch their young, in or about altars, which were kept in a state of the *greatest purity*, and where *perpetual fires* were kept for the purpose of sacrifice, burning incense, etc.” He proposes to read the words beginning at the third verse and ending with *her young ones*, within a parenthesis, and to explain the remaining part of the verse as the conclusion of the sentence commencing at verse 2d; or to read the parenthesis as the close of verse 3d: “Even the sparrow hath found out a house, and the swallow (ring-dove) a nest for herself, where she may lay her young; *but I have no place either of rest or worship*.” But though it cannot be reasonably supposed that these birds would be permitted to nestle about the altar itself, before which the priests were continually serving; yet it is not improbable that they were permitted to construct their nests in the houses near the altar. “The altar,” says Dr Paxton, “is here by a synecdoche of a part for the whole, to be understood of the tabernacle, among the rafters of which, the sparrow and the swallow were allowed to nestle; or rather for the buildings which surrounded the sacred edifice where the priests and their assistants had their ordinary residence.” — *Paxton*’*s Illustrations of Scripture*, volume 2, pages 310, 355. Dr Morison, after quoting the criticism of Dr Clarke, observes, “I confess I see a great beauty in adhering to the sense given in the common version. Though the sparrow and ring-dove are represented as finding a nest for themselves at the altars of the sanctuary, it does not follow that the inspired writer intends any thing more than that, while he was exiled from the house of his God, these familiar birds had a home near that sacred spot where he had associated his chief joys.” Parkhurst considers, that a comparison is intended; and that though the particles of similitude “as” and “so” are not in the Hebrew text, they are to be understood. And in the Hebrew Scriptures, there are many instances in which they are omitted, but where it is necessary to supply them to make an intelligible version. He translates as follows: “Even (as) the sparrow findeth her house, and the dove her nest, where she hath laid her young, (so, should I find,) thy altars, O Jehovah of Hosts! my King, and my God.” According to this exposition, the Psalmist illustrates his vehement longing after the sacred tabernacle, and God’s public worship, by the natural affection of birds, and by that joy and delight with which they return to their brood after they have been absent from them. (See Parkhurst’s Lexicon on <span style="color:#008080">rrd</span>,2.) Walford takes the same view. His version is: —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">“As the sparrow findeth a house, and the swallow a nest,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Where she may place her offspring,</span> <span style="color:#000080">So may thy altars be my abode, O Jehovah of Hosts!</span> <span style="color:#000080">My King, and my God.”</span></p>
